Title: 

Authors: Unknown
Published: 
Categories: 2
ArXiv ID: hf_6663

Abstract:
SentRNA: Improving computational RNA design by incorporating a prior of human design strategies Jade Shi, EteRNA players, Rhiju Das, and Vijay S. Pande Abstract: Designing RNA sequences that fold into specific structures and perform desired biological functions is an emerging field in bioengineering with broad applications from intracellular chemical catalysis to cancer therapy via selective gene silencing. Effective RNA design requires first solving the inverse folding problem: given a target structure, propose a sequence that folds into that structure. Although significant progress has been made in developing computational algorithms for this purpose, current approaches are ineffective at designing sequences for complex targets, limiting their utility in real-world applications. However, an alternative that has shown significantly higher performance are human players of the online RNA design game EteRNA. Through many rounds of gameplay, these players have developed a collective library of "human" rules and strategies for RNA design that have proven to be more effective than current computational approaches, especially for complex targets. Here, we present an RNA design agent, SentRNA, which consists of a fully-connected neural network trained using the eternasolves dataset, a set of 1.8 x 104 player-submitted sequences across 724 unique targets. The agent first predicts an initial sequence for a target using the trained network, and then refines that solution if necessary using a short adaptive walk utilizing a canon of standard design moves. Through this approach, we observe SentRNA can learn and apply human-like design strategies to solve several complex targets previously unsolvable by any computational approach. We thus demonstrate that incorporating a prior of human design strategies into a computational agent can significantly boost its performance, and suggests a new paradigm for machine-based RNA design. Introduction: Solving the inverse folding problem for RNA is a critical prerequisite to effective RNA design, an emerging field of modern bioengineering research. 1,2,3,4,5 A RNA molecule's function is highly dependent on the structure into which it folds, which in turn is determined by the sequence of nucleotides that comprise it. Therefore, designing RNA molecules to perform specific functions requires designing sequences that fold into specific structures. As such, significant efforts have been made over the past several decades in developing computational algorithms to reliably predict RNA sequences that fold into a given target.6,7,8,9,10,11 Existing computational methods for inverse RNA folding can be roughly separated into two types. The first type generates an initial guess of a sequence and then refines the sequence using some form of stochastic search. Published algorithms that fall under this category include RNAInverse,6 RNA-SSD,7 INFO-RNA,8 NUPACK,10 and MODENA.11 RNAInverse, one of the first inverse folding algorithms, initializes the sequence randomly and then uses a simple adaptive walk in which random single or pair mutations are successively performed, and a mutation is accepted if it improves the structural similarity between the current and the target structure. RNA-SSD first performs hierarchical decomposition of the target and then performs adaptive walk separately on each substructure to reduce the size of the search space. INFO-RNA first generates an initial guess of the sequence using dynamic programming to estimate the minimum energy sequence for a target structure, and then performs simulated annealing on the sequence.  NUPACK performs hierarchical decomposition of the target and assigns an initial sequence to each substructure. For each sequence, it then generates a thermodynamic ensemble of possible structures and stochastically perturbs the sequence to optimize the "ensemble defect" term, which represents the average number of improperly paired bases relative to the target over the entire ensemble. Finally, one of the most recent algorithms, MODENA, generates an ensemble of initial sequences using a genetic algorithm, and then performs stochastic search using crossovers and single-point mutations. The second type of design algorithm, exemplified by programs such as DSS-Opt, foregoes stochastic search and instead attempts to generate a valid sequence directly from gradient-based optimization. Given a target, DSS-Opt generates an initial sequence and then performs a gradient-based optimization of an objective function that includes the predicted free energy of the target and a "negative design" term that punishes improperly paired bases. Both types of algorithms have proven effective given simple to moderately complex structures. However, there is still much room for improvement. A recent benchmark of these algorithms showed that they consistently fail given large or structurally complex targets, 12 limiting their applicability to designing RNA molecules for real-world biological applications. A promising alternative approach to RNA design that has consistently outperformed current computational methods is EteRNA, a web-based graphical interface in which the RNA design problem is presented to humans as a game. 13 Players of the game are shown 2D representations of target RNA structures ("puzzles") and asked to propose sequences that fold into them. These sequences are first judged using the ViennaRNA 1.8.5 software package 6 and then validated experimentally. Through this cycle of design and evaluation, players build a collective library of design strategies that can then be applied to new, more complex puzzles. These strategies are distinct from those employed by design algorithms such as DSS-Opt and NUPACK in that they are honed through visual pattern recognition and experience. Remarkably, these human-developed strategies have proven more effective for RNA design than current computational methods. For example, EteRNA players significantly outperform even the best computational algorithms on the Eterna100, a set of 100 challenging puzzles designed by EteRNA players to showcase a variety of RNA structural elements that make design difficult. While top-ranking human players can solve all 100 puzzles, even the best-scoring computational algorithm, MODENA, could only solve 54 / 100 puzzles. 12 Given the success of these strategies, we decided to investigate whether incorporating these strategies into a computational agent can increase its performance beyond that of current state-of-the-art methods. In this study, we present SentRNA, a computational agent for RNA design that significantly outperforms existing computational algorithms by learning human-like design strategies in a data driven manner. The agent consists of a fully-connected neural network that takes as input a featurized representation of the local environment around a given position in a puzzle. The output is length-4, corresponding to the four RNA nucleotides (bases): A, U, C, or G. The model is trained using the eternasolves dataset, a custom-compiled collection of 1.8 x 10 4 playersubmitted solutions across 724 unique puzzles. These puzzles comprise both the “Progression” puzzles, designed for beginning EteRNA players, as well as several “Lab” puzzles for which solutions were experimentally synthesized and tested. During validation and testing the agent takes an initially blank puzzle and assigns bases to every position greedily based on the output values. If this initial prediction is not valid, as judged by ViennaRNA 1.8.5, it is further refined via  an adaptive walk using a canon of standard design moves compiled by players and taught to new players through the game's puzzle progression. Overall, we trained and tested an ensemble of 165 models, each using a distinct training set and model input (see Methods). Collectively, the ensemble of models can solve 42 / 100 puzzles from the Eterna100 by neural network prediction alone, and 80 / 100 puzzles using neural network prediction + refinement. Among these 80 puzzles are all 15 puzzles highlighted during a previous benchmark by Anderson Lee et al. 12 Notably, among these 15 puzzles are 7 puzzles yet unsolvable by any computational algorithm. This study demonstrates that teaching human design strategies to a computational RNA design agent in a data-driven manner can lead to significant increases in performance over previous methods, and represents a new paradigm in machine-based RNA design in which both human and computational design strategies are united into a single agent. Methods: Code availability: The source code for SentRNA, all our trained models, and the full eternasolves dataset can be found on GitHub: https://github.com/jadeshi/SentRNA. Hardware: We performed all computations (training, validation, testing, and refinement) using a desktop computer with an Intel Core i7-6700K @ 4.00 GHz CPU and 16 GB of RAM. Creating 2D structural representations of puzzles: During training and testing of almost all models, we used the RNAplot function from ViennaRNA 1.8.5 to render puzzles as 2D structures given their dot-bracket representations. However, when training and testing two specific models M6 and M8 on two highly symmetric puzzles, “Mat - Lot 2-2 B" and “Mutated chicken feet” (see Results and Discussion), we decided to use an in-house rendering algorithm (hereafter called EteRNA rendering) in place of RNAplot, as we found the RNAplot was unable to properly render the symmetric structure of the puzzles. Neural network architecture: Our goal is to create an RNA design agent that can propose a sequence of RNA bases that folds into a given target structure, i.e. fill in an initially blank EteRNA puzzle. To do this, we employ a fully connected neural network that assigns an identity of A, U, C, or G to each position in the puzzle given a featurized representation of its local environment. During test time, we expose the agent to every position in the puzzle sequentially and have it predict its identity. The neural network was implemented using TensorFlow14 and contains three hidden layers of 100 nodes with ReLU nonlinearities. The output is length-4, corresponding to the four RNA bases: A, U, C, and G. During validation and test time, base identities are assigned greedily to the puzzle based on these output values. Given a position x in the puzzle, the input for this position to the agent is a combination of information about its bonding partner, nearest neighbors, and long-range features, which can include, for example, next nearest neighbors or adjacent closing pairs in a multiloop. While the bonding partner and nearest neighbor information is provided to the agent by default, long-range features are learned through the training data.  The information about the bonding partner is encoded as a length-5 vector, with each position in the vector representing either A, U, C, G, or "none" (i.e. a blank position that does not have a base assigned to it yet). A value of 1 is assigned to the position corresponding to the identity of the bonding partner, while all other values are set to 0. If there is no bonding partner, all values are set to 0. The nearest neighbor information is encoded as a length-11 vector, a combination of two length-5 onehots corresponding to the identities of the bases directly before and after it in the sequence, and a single value that corresponds to the angle in radians formed by the base and its nearest neighbors. This angle serves to distinguish bases belonging to different substructures in the puzzle. For example, a base situated in the middle of a large internal loop will have a larger angle than a base positioned in a 4-loop. As a design choice, any position in the middle of a stack of bonded bases was assigned an angle of 0. Also, if the model is looking at either the first or last position in the puzzle, the “before” and “after” nearest neighbor portions of the input respectively are set to 0. Long-range features refer to important positions y in the puzzle relative to x that the agent should also have knowledge of when deciding what base to assign to x. These are each defined by a set of two values: 1) the Cartesian distance, L, between x and y in the puzzle given the 2D rendering of the puzzle, and 2) the angle in radians, Φ, formed by positions x – 1, x, and y. These two values are stored in a list, [L, Φ], and serve as a label for the feature. For example, when using RNAplot, a label of [15.0, 1.6] corresponds to a base's bonding partner in the middle of a stem. The bonding distance is equal to 15.0 RNAplot distance units, and the angle between the previous base in the stem, the current base, and the bonding partner is 1.6 radians, or 90 degrees. A length-5 vector of zeros is then appended to the input vector to serve as a placeholder for the feature. During training, validation, or testing, when the agent is looking at a given position x, it computes L and Φ between x and other positions yi in the puzzle, and if both L and Φ match that of a long-range feature used in the model within some threshold, a 1 is assigned to the corresponding placeholder depending on the identity of yi (A, U, C, G, or “none”). We set the threshold for both L and Φ to 0.1 (distance units and radians respectively) when using RNAplot, and a stricter value of 10-5 for both L and Φ when using EteRNA rendering. We determine what long-range features to use (i.e. which features should be considered "important") using a mutual information metric over player solutions. First, we perform a pairwise mutual information calculation using all the player solutions for a given puzzle to form a 𝑙 x 𝑙 mutual information matrix, where 𝑙 is the length of the puzzle. We then select the top M (user-defined) positions in the matrix with highest mutual information, and for each of these positions (x, y) compute L and Φ to give a list of long-range features for the puzzle (Figure 1). This process is repeated for each puzzle, and the unique long-range features across all the puzzles are then combined into an aggregate list of long-range features. A random subset of N (user-defined) features is then selected from this list and used to define a model to be used for training, validation, and testing. By defining long-range features using a mutual information metric, our goal is to impose a prior of human knowledge onto the agent. High mutual information between positions x and y indicates that the identity of position x is strongly correlated to that of position y. In other words, when EteRNA players are choosing in-game what to assign for x, they are first typically looking at y, or vice versa. Therefore, by only including positions with high mutual information to the agent’s field of vision, the agent prioritizes what humans have deemed to be important. As a result, we  provide the model with enough information to prevent underfitting and enable it to apply its learned strategies to more difficult puzzles. On the other hand, we also limit the model complexity such that we can train the model using a relatively small number of training examples without overfitting. For example, all models in this study were trained using a maximum of 50 player solutions. Training algorithm: We used subsets of the first 721 / 724 puzzles from eternasolves to train the model, puzzles 722 and 724 for initial validation and testing respectively, and the Eterna100 for more extensive testing. Puzzle 723 was skipped due to being completely unstructured and not useful for validation. We confirmed that there was no contamination between the training, validation, and test sets. Because there is no straightforward way to determine a priori what long-range features and training examples will result in the best-performing model, we decided to train and test an extensive ensemble of models. To do this, we first computed an aggregate list of 42 long-range features using all puzzles from eternasolves with at least 50 submitted solutions, allowing each puzzle to contribute only one long-range feature (M = 1). We set this threshold of 50 player solutions since puzzles with a small number of solutions can introduce noise into the mutual information calculation. We then randomly selected a subset of long-range features from the aggregate list and built a model using these features. We built one model each using between 0 to 42 randomly chosen features, and repeated this process 20 times to build a total of 43 x 20 = 860 models. To form the training sets for these models, we first randomly chose 50 puzzles from eternasolves to serve as training puzzles. Each model then randomly sampled one solution from each of these puzzles to give a training set of 50 player solutions. In addition to the above 860 models, we built another 256 models that scanned more widely over hyperparameters. For these models, we computed the aggregate list of long-range features using all puzzles, not just those with 50 player solutions or more, and loosened the restriction on the number of long-range features that could be contributed by each puzzle (M = 1 to 4). This expanded the number of features included in the models to a maximum of 312. For each model, we picked a random set of 50 puzzles from eternasolves to serve as the training puzzles, and also allowed the model to select multiple player solutions from each puzzle to form the training set (n = 1 to 8). To train each model, we use the following procedure. For each player solution in our training set, we first visually render the corresponding puzzle using RNAplot. We then set the identity of every position in the puzzle to the corresponding base in the player solution, and featurize each position into bonding pair, nearest neighbors, and long-range features to form the input vector. The output label is set to the identity of the corresponding position in the player solution. Then, we decompose the player solution into a "solution trajectory" to teach the agent how to solve an initially blank puzzle with no bases assigned (i.e. during test time). This is done by first removing all base assignments from the puzzle. A position in the puzzle is then selected and featurized. All input features (bonding partner, nearest neighbors, long-range features) are at this point set to “none”, and the output label is set to the identity of the corresponding position in the player solution. This position is then filled in with the player solution base, and the next position is picked and featurized (Figure 1). This process continues until all positions in the puzzle  have been featurized. The order in which puzzle positions are filled in during this process can be either sequential or stochastic (user-defined). By doing this, we are mimicking the process of a human player filling in the puzzle base by base and training the model to reproduce these steps. During validation and testing, the agent proceeds through each position in the (initially blank) puzzle sequentially and assigns bases greedily based on the model outputs. For our first batch of 860 models, we only used sequential fill-in. However, for the second batch of 256 models, we allowed both sequential and stochastic fill-in. We initialize each model using Gaussian weights (µ=0, σ=0.02), unit biases, and a learning rate of 0.001. We train each model using the Adam optimizer 15 for a total of 1000 epochs, performing a validation on puzzle 722 every 100 epochs, to give a total of 10 models. The model with the highest validation accuracy is then used for testing on puzzle 724. During validation and testing, we allowed the model two attempts at predicting a sequence, once using a blank sequence as input, and again using the initial model-predicted sequence as input. The second attempt is intended as an opportunity for the model to refine its first prediction. If the model proposed valid solutions for both validation and test puzzles, it was then subjected to more extensive testing on the Eterna100. Finally, we built and trained two models using hand-picked training puzzles from eternasolves to allow the model to learn rarely seen, advanced strategies necessary to solve two puzzles from the Eterna100, Mat – Lot 2-2 B and Mutated chicken feet. These models were not tested on the full Eterna100, but only on their respective puzzles. In total, we trained a total of 860 + 256 + 2 = 1118 models, tested 163 of these models on the full Eterna100, and tested 2 models specifically on Mat – Lot 2-2 B and Mutated chicken feet, for a total of 163 + 2 = 165 models tested.  Figure 1: The training and validation procedure for SentRNA consists of first selecting a puzzle, and then performing a pairwise mutual information calculation using all player solutions for that puzzle. Positions in the resulting mutual information matrix with high values are then used to define new long-range features that will be included in the model’s field of vision (steps 1-3). These features are appended to the base input vector that by default has information about the bonding partner and nearest neighbors. SentRNA is then trained to reproduce a player solution at each position in the puzzle. To train the model, we use a two-part training set consisting of both the full player solution as well as a synthetic “solution trajectory” to simulate the process of solving a puzzle base-by-base starting from a blank puzzle (step 4). During validation and testing, the model is exposed to each position in a new (initially blank) puzzle sequentially and greedily fills in bases one-by-one based on the model outputs (step 5). Refinement algorithm: During testing on the Eterna100, if the initial predicted solution does not fold into the target structure, as judged by Vienna 1.8.5, we further refine this solution using an adaptive walk. We use the following refinement moves: 1) pairing two unpaired bases that should be paired in the target structure, 2) re-pairing two paired bases that should be paired, 3) unpairing two paired bases that should not be paired, and 4) G or U-U boosting,16 two common stabilization strategies taught to beginning EteRNA players. During refinement, random trajectories of these moves are generated and applied to the initial sequence until one that folds into the target structure is reached. At any point, if an intermediate sequence is reached that folds into a structure more structurally similar to the target, which we define as the fraction of matching characters in the dot-bracket notations, the refinement trajectory ends and all subsequent trajectories begin from that point. Unless otherwise noted (see Results and Discussion), we limited the refinement to 300 trajectories of length 30, which takes at most 90 seconds for most puzzles in the Eterna100 (~100 bases or fewer in length). By comparison, all algorithms tested in the previous benchmark by Anderson-Lee et al. were given a much longer time limit of 24 hours. 12 We also investigated the ability of the refinement algorithm to solve puzzles on its own without the benefit of the initial neural network prediction by repeating the refinement process for all the Eterna100 puzzles while starting from initial sequences generated by two common initialization methods used in previous computational algorithms (see Results and Discussion, Table 2). Testing statistical robustness of results: Over the course of testing models on the Eterna100, we noticed specific models that seemed particularly capable at solving difficult puzzles, solving them quickly after only a few minutes of refinement (see Results and Discussion). This leads to the apparent conclusion that these models are well-trained and provide useful prior information toward solving these puzzles. However, an alternative explanation is that by cherry-picking and looking at only a successful model, we are ignoring the possibility that the "success" of that one model may simply be by chance, the result of subjecting poorly trained models to many independent rounds of refinement. Therefore, to establish a quantitative metric of performance, for each model we will discuss, we repeated the refinement from the same initial model prediction n times and defined the refinement efficacy Eref as the fraction of times the puzzle is successfully solved. We also estimated the expected  refinement time Tref by dividing the cumulative time tall taken for all trials by the number of successes, nsuccess. This represents the average amount of unproductive time elapsed before a valid solution is sampled. Error bars for Tref can be computed from the standard error for Eref, which is: 𝜎  =  (𝐸  −𝐸 𝑛 /  )  /  This can be used to set error bounds on Eref and nsuccess: 𝐸 𝐸  = 𝐸 = 𝐸 𝑛 𝑛  −𝜎 +𝜎 = 𝐸 = 𝐸  ∗ 𝑛 ∗ 𝑛  Finally, we can use these to estimate error bounds for Tref:  𝜎  ±  𝑇  =  𝑇  =  = [𝑇  𝑡 𝑛  𝑡  𝑛 −𝑇  ,𝑇  −𝑇  ]  A Tref less than 24 hours (8.64 x 104 s) after accounting for error would demonstrate that the model is indeed making intelligent initial predictions of the solution and outperforming previous methods. Results and Discussion: Overall performance of SentRNA on Eterna100 In total, SentRNA can solve 80 / 100 puzzles from the Eterna100 across all 163 + 2 models trained and tested (Table 1). Notably, of these 80 puzzles, SentRNA can solve 42 through its initial neural network prediction alone (i.e. no refinement), indicating that the strategies learned by SentRNA from the eternasolves training set are indeed directly generalizable to more difficult puzzles and can constitute a useful prior for design.  Table 1: Performance of the SentRNA ensemble of 163 + 2 trained models on the full set of Eterna100 puzzles. An "X" in the “Pred” column means that a model could predict a correct solution to the puzzle without refinement, and an "X" in the "Ref" column means the model was able to solve the puzzle after refinement. The number in the "Models" column represents the total number of models that could solve the puzzle. In general, puzzles were solved using default training and refinement parameters: 50 randomly selected player solutions for training, and 300 adaptive walk trajectories of length 30 using the full moveset for refinement. However, three puzzles, Crop circle 2, Mat – Lot 2-2 B, and Mutated chicken feet, denoted by the "X*" next to the name, could only be solved using training and/or refinement conditions different from the default (see Results and Discussion). We also tested the ability of the adaptive walk refinement to solve puzzles on its own without the benefit of SentRNA's neural network prediction as a prior (hereafter called SentRNA initialization). For every puzzle in the Eterna100, we repeated the refinement process starting from the following initializations: 1) setting all base pairs in the target structure to GC pairs and all other bases to A's (hereafter called GC initialization) and 2) assigning random bases to the full  sequence (hereafter called random initialization). To perform a fair comparison to the results from SentRNA initialization, we first computed the overall refinement efficacy given SentRNA initialization, Eref, by dividing the number of models capable of solving each puzzle (Table 1) by 163, the total number of models tested. We then performed 1 / E ref refinement trials for each puzzle using both random and GC initialization. For example, for the puzzle Kudzu, which was solved by 4 trained models out of 163, Eref = 4 / 163, and therefore we performed 163 / 4 = 40 refinement trials beginning from both GC and random initialization. For puzzles SentRNA was unable to solve, we performed 163 refinement trials from both GC and random initialization. Each trial consisted of 300 trajectories of length 30 using the full refinement moveset. The results are shown in Table 2.  Table 2: Solved puzzles are denoted with an "X". The "Random init." column corresponds to the results starting from random initialization, "GC init" corresponds to the results starting from GC initialization, and the "SentRNA" column, a reproduction of the results shown in Table 1, corresponds to the results starting from SentRNA initialization. SentRNA initialization overall  proves most effective, solving the largest number of puzzles (80 / 100), followed by GC initialization (69 / 100), and random initialization (21 / / 100). We observe that although refinement beginning from random initialization performs poorly, solving only 21 / 100 puzzles, refinement beginning GC initialization performs surprisingly well, solving 69 / 100 puzzles. This indicates that even when paired with a simple initialization method, adaptive walk refinement using a moveset of simple human design strategies is sufficient to greatly surpass the limit of current computational algorithms (54 / 100 puzzles solved by MODENA).12 We hypothesize that incorporating more advanced moves and strategies into the moveset could further improve the performance of the adaptive walk algorithm, and may be a promising direction for future machine-based RNA design. Performance of SentRNA on difficult structural design elements Anderson-Lee et al. previously highlighted several representative puzzles from the Eterna100 with structural features that make computational RNA design difficult. 12 Here, we will discuss our agent’s performance on these puzzles in comparison with previous methods. Specifically, we will discuss five types of puzzles containing the following features: 1) short stems, 2) internal loops / bulges, 3) trivial solution strategy, 4) rarely seen structural motifs, and 5) symmetric elements. For each puzzle, we will examine the performance of one specific model out of the ensemble that succeeded in solving the puzzle. The expected refinement times, 𝑇 𝜎  −𝜎  ,𝑇  +  , for each model discussed are summarized in Table 3. For interested readers, all  models we discuss are available on the SentRNA repository on Github, and directory paths for each model, listed by puzzle name, are provided in the file “good_models.txt”. 1) Short stems Puzzles containing short stems are in general difficult to design because the stems are energetically unstable. Furthermore, the design difficulty increases as the number of stems increase, or if the stems are arranged in a symmetric way.12 As a result, several puzzles in the Eterna100 with these features proved difficult or impossible to solve by existing computational algorithms. In contrast, we find that SentRNA is successful at addressing these challenges. Two representative puzzles, Shortie 4 and Shortie 6, both of which RNAInverse failed to solve,12 were solved without refinement (Figure 2, top). In one representative case, SentRNA assigns alternating GC/CG pairs to all stems. Furthermore, it also mutates the 5' end of every 4-loop to a G, a human design strategy known as "boosting" that is known to stabilize stems,16 and in this case, was critical for stabilizing the short stems in both puzzles. Most notably, SentRNA could also solve a set of two representative puzzles that none of the previous computational algorithms could solve, Kyurem 7 and Kyurem 5. The large number of short stems in the puzzles, combined with the fact that many of these stems are arranged around multiloops, make these structures significantly more difficult to solve compared to the Shortie puzzles.12 To solve these puzzles, it is necessary to introduce asymmetries to the base assignments for the stems, as identical base assignments promote mispairing between stems. 12 This is in stark contrast to the Shortie puzzles, in which simply boosting the 4-loops was sufficient to stabilize the structure, even in the presence of identical base assignments.  Remarkably, we observe that SentRNA can propose such asymmetric stem designs even before refinement. For example, one trained model that solved Kyurem 7, renamed here M1 for simplicity, assigns bases to all three stems around the first multiloop differently (Figure 2, middle). Stem 1 is arranged CG/UA/GC, stem 2 is GC/UA/CG, and stem 3 is GC/AU/CG. The 4-loop and 5loop are also boosted by a G at the 5' end. This solution can be quickly refined into a valid solution through a series of four pair mutations: two GC, one AU, and one GU. Similarly, a model that solved Kyurem 5, renamed here M2, proposed an initial solution with asymmetric bonding patterns for the stems (Figure 2, bottom). For example, stems 1 and 3 consist of three GC pairs, whereas stem 2 consists of an AU pair sandwiched between 2 GC pairs. Upon closer inspection, stems 1 and 3 also have distinct bonding patterns, with stem 1 being CG/CG/GC, and stem 3 being GC/CG/GC. The 4-loop in the puzzle is also boosted. This initial solution can be successfully refined after a sequence of GC and AU pair mutations and swaps. However, compared to Kyurem 7, Kyurem 5 required more effort to refine, using more than twice the number of refinement moves. Interestingly, the fact that SentRNA has more difficulty solving Kyurem 5 is consistent with the fact that historically, fewer successful solutions have been proposed by players for Kyurem 5 than Kyurem 7.12 Given that only pair mutations are necessary to refine both Kyurem 7 and Kyurem 5 beginning from their initial predictions, we repeated the refinement process 100 times for both puzzles using only pair mutation moves to obtain more comprehensive statistics, and computed the expected refinement times as outlined in Methods (Table 3). We find that Kyurem 7 can be refined in ~2 minutes, whereas Kyurem 5 requires a longer time of ~10 minutes, both well within the 24 hours allowed by Anderson-Lee et al.12 Interestingly, we also observed that adaptive walk refinement on its own beginning from GC initialization, using only pair mutation moves, could solve both these puzzles, albeit less efficiently than starting from SentRNA initialization (Table 4). These results reiterate the results from Table 2 and again suggest that adaptive walk on its own is a promising strategy for design.  Figure 2: SentRNA is competent at solving puzzles containing short stems, solving Shortie 4 and Shortie 6 (top) without refinement, and both Kyurem 7 (middle) and Kyurem 5 (bottom) after refinement. Kyurem 7 requires only four pair mutations, 2 GC, 1 AU, and 1 GU (highlighted, red) to solve. Kyurem 5 requires a larger number of refinement moves consisting of both GC and AU pair mutations (highlighted, red). In summary, SentRNA is capable of learning asymmetric design strategies for short stems, and as such can propose nearly valid solutions for difficult puzzles that can then be successfully refined via a short adaptive walk. Furthermore, the moveset needed to refine both Kyurem 5 and Kyurem 7 was simple, consisting of only pair mutations (i.e. no unpairing or boosting), making it directly comparable to the algorithm used by RNAInverse.6 The fact that SentRNA can quickly solve these puzzles using simple refinement moves employed in previous methods demonstrates that the design strategies it learns are indeed useful for solving difficult puzzles. 2) Bulges and internal loops Adding bulges and internal loops into a puzzle decreases the amount of overall base pairing and destabilizes the structure, increasing design difficulty. However, we observe that SentRNA can readily solve several representative puzzles that exhibit features which previous methods struggled to or could not solve.  Two representative puzzles containing bulges were solved easily with no or very little refinement. The puzzle just down to 1 bulge, unsolvable by RNAInverse and RNA-SSD12 was solved without refinement (Figure 3, top), and "1,2,3and4bulges", solvable only by MODENA,12 needed only a single GC swap beginning from an initial prediction from an appropriately trained model, renamed here M3. During the refinement trajectory, an additional boosting move was made to the first multiloop, but this was not necessary to solve the puzzle. We observed that the key stabilizing mutation necessary to solving this puzzle was already learned: boosting a 4-loop to stabilize the adjacent length-1 stem, which is particularly unstable due to its short length (Figure 3, bottom). Like the Kyurem puzzles, because only pair mutation moves are necessary to refine "1,2,3and4bulges", we repeated the refinement 100 times using only pair mutation moves and calculated expected refinement times for the puzzle, which are reported in Table 3. We find that this puzzle can be refined quickly in ~20 seconds due to the intelligent initialization of the sequence by SentRNA. Like the Kyurem puzzles, we observe that adaptive walk refinement beginning from GC initialization is sufficient to solve "1,2,3and4bulges" (Table 2). However, unlike the Kyurem puzzles, for which a moveset of only pair mutations was sufficient to solve the puzzles, to solve "1,2,3and4bulges", boosting moves must be included as part of the moveset, as otherwise the critical 4-loop attached to the length-1 stem cannot be stabilized (Table 4). By comparison, as our results with M3 demonstrate, teaching the 4-loop boosting strategy to SentRNA directly through the training data proves to be a significantly more efficient solution strategy.  Figure 3: Puzzles containing bulges are solved quickly by SentRNA, with just down to 1 bulge (top) requiring no refinement (top), and “1,2,3and4bulges” (bottom) requiring only a single GC base pair swap (highlighted, red). The boost to the first multiloop (highlighted, blue) that was also added is unnecessary to solve the puzzle. In contrast, we found that SentRNA struggled with puzzles containing large internal loops that had to be stabilized by boosting. Loop next to a Multiloop, solved only by RNA-SSD,12 required substantially more refinement steps to solve than “1,2,3and4bulges”. The key stabilizations needed to solve the puzzle, all added by refinement, consisted of a series of boosts: one U-U and 1 G boost to the large 7-7 internal loop, and two G boosts to the 1-1-1 multiloop (Figure 4, top). We observed that the trained model, renamed here M4, was unable to learn any internal loop boosting strategies from the training data. This is due to the scarcity of puzzles with these structural features in the eternasolves training set. Only 79 solutions (0.04% of the training set) corresponded to a puzzle with a 7-7 internal loop. In contrast, the model could learn the much more common G-boost for 4-loops and boost both 4-loops in the puzzle. However, this was not sufficient to compensate for the lack of internal loop boosting. To obtain more comprehensive statistics of the refinement efficacy for Loop next to a Multiloop, we calculated  Tref over 120 independent refinement trials using the full refinement moveset (Table 3). Interestingly, we find that although SentRNA was unable to learn the necessary boosting strategies during training, the adapative walk refinement can effectively compensate for SentRNA’s lack of prior knowledge and rapidly sample the necessary moves, solving the puzzle in under 5 minutes. SentRNA faced similar challenges when trying to solve Crop circle 2, a difficult puzzle consisting of many large internal loops that must each be boosted. This puzzle was unsolvable by any previous computational algorithm.12 Because SentRNA has difficulty learning internal loop boosting strategies from eternasolves, a model that could solve Crop circle 2, renamed here M5, was initially only able to assign GC pairs to all bonded bases in the puzzle (Figure 4, bottom). All internal loop boosts had to be added during refinement, resulting in an average refinement time of ~1 hour over 40 independent refinement trials, significantly longer than the time needed to solve “1,2,3and4bulges” and Loop next to a Multiloop (Table 3). In fact, to solve Crop circle 2, it was necessary to modify the default refinement procedure in two ways: 1) use 10000 refinement trajectories instead of the default 300 to accommodate the larger search space of the puzzle, and 2) simplify the refinement moveset to only GC pair mutations and boosts such that boosting moves would be sampled more often. This result demonstrates that if SentRNA is unable to learn relevant solution strategies directly through training, its performance can drop significantly, and relying on refinement to reach a valid solution can quickly become an untenable strategy as puzzle length and complexity increases. As a further demonstration of this fact, Crop circle 2 can be solved much more efficiently beginning from GC initialization using the same refinement parameters (Table 4). This result indicates that the GC pair initialization predicted using M5 was suboptimal, a consequence of uninformative training data leading to an uninformative initial prediction.  Figure 4: SentRNA has difficulty with puzzles with large multiloops that must be stabilized via boosting. Loop next to a Multiloop (top) consists of a particularly unstable structural motif of an internal loop and multiloop connected by a single base pair, and as a result three different boosts and two pair mutations (highlighted, red) must be done during refinement to solve the puzzle. Likewise, Crop circle 2 consists of several internal loops that must each be boosted. Since SentRNA could not learn these strategies, it relied on refinement to sample the 9 necessary boosting moves, substantially increasing the required refinement time. On the other hand, if SentRNA can learn the needed solution strategies directly through an intelligently selected training set, it shows good performance even for very large puzzles. Mat - Lot 2-2 B is a puzzle of length 284, more than twice the size of Crop circle 2, that consists of  several 2-2 internal loops to which an advanced UGUG boosting strategy must be applied. Only 14.9% of the training set have 2-2 loops, and of these solutions only 21.7% utilize this strategy. We found that a training set randomly sampled from the full set of player solutions did not allow the agent to learn this strategy, resulting in failure to solve the puzzle. Refinement was also not effective because this strategy was not included as a refinement move, not to mention the prohibitively large search space given the puzzle’s long length. However, we can significantly improve SentRNA’s performance by limiting the training set to player solutions that abundantly exhibit this boosting strategy. Specifically, instead of picking random puzzle solutions from eternasolves to use as training data, we picked one solution each from a set of 11 puzzles with "2-2" in the name from eternasolves. Using this training set, we could train a model, renamed here M6, that could reliably boost all the appropriate 2-2 loops in Mat - Lot 2-2 B. Subsequent refinement, which we limited to only GC pair mutations, were sufficient to solve the puzzle (Figure 5). When training and testing M6, we chose to use EteRNA rendering instead of RNAplot to generate 2D structures, as we found RNAplot was unable to properly render the highly symmetric nature of Mat - Lot 2-2 B. Expected refinement times for Mat - Lot 2-2 B, computed over 140 independent refinement trials using only GC pair mutation moves, are reported in Table 3. We observe that despite its large size, Mat - Lot 2-2 B can be solved quickly by refinement in ~2 minutes due to SentRNA's ability to learn the UGUG 2-2 boost during training. On the other hand, if the UGUG boost is not applied in the initial sequence, the puzzle cannot be solved by refinement (Table 4).  Figure 5: To solve Mat – Lot 2-2 B, one must boost all 2-2 internal loops in the puzzle with an advanced, rarely seen UGUG boosting strategy. Randomly sampling from the training data did not allow SentRNA to learn this strategy, but limiting the training set to only 11 puzzles from eternasolves with “2-2” in the name, in which the boosting strategy is abundantly used, allowed  SentRNA to learn and apply the strategy. This allows the puzzle to be refined quickly via a series of GC mutations (highlighted, red). Overall, these results demonstrate that the quality of the training data is critical in determining the efficacy of SentRNA. Informative training data can teach the necessary strategies to solving a puzzle, after which the puzzle can be solved quickly using a simple refinement algorithm of only GC pair mutations and swaps (e.g. “1,2,3and4bulges”, Mat – Lot 2-2 B). On the other hand, uninformative training data that cannot provide examples of necessary strategies result in the need for more advanced refinement moves such as internal loop boosting and longer refinement times (e.g. Crop circle 2). 3) Trivial solution strategy Many computational algorithms, such as RNA-Inverse and RNA-SSD, first initialize a sequence randomly and then perform stochastic refinement on it. This can lead to difficulty solving puzzles such as This is ACTUALLY small and easy 6, which is a sequence of 400 unpaired bases that has a trivial solution of setting every position to the same base. While this solution is immediately obvious to humans, the random sequence initialization employed by many computational algorithms results in unintended base pairs that must all be simultaneously unpaired through the stochastic refinement. Surprisingly, this unpairing is not a straightforward task; all methods surveyed by Anderson-Lee et al. except NUPACK and DSS-Opt failed to solve this puzzle. 12 In contrast, our approach, which is based on learning human design strategies, is well-suited to solving this puzzle. In fact, it is common for a trained model to initially propose the "human" solution of setting all 400 bases to A (i.e. doing nothing). 4) Rarely seen structural motifs Structural motifs that are rarely seen in nature can introduce significant design challenges for computational algorithms. For example, the puzzle hard Y, which consists of an unusual structural motif of two consecutive length-1 bulges (the “zigzag”), was unsolvable by any previous computational algorithm.12 We observed that this structural motif also presented difficulties for SentRNA. A model capable of eventually solving hard Y after refinement, renamed here M7, initially failed to stabilize the zigzag portion of the puzzle. During refinement, a series of GC pair swaps to the zigzag and the multiloop, and mutation of the second bulge base of the zigzag to C, were made to solve the puzzle (Figure 6). Interestingly, because we do not include single base mutations as part of the refinement moveset, this mutation of this base could have only been accomplished via an un-pairing move. This observation suggests that during the refinement process, this base was prone to mispairing, likely due to the instability of the zigzag structure, and mutating the base to C was necessary to inhibit this behavior. Expected refinement times for hard Y, computed over 220 independent refinement trials using the full refinement moveset, are reported in Table 3. Interestingly, we observe that although the zigzag is an unfamiliar structural feature to SentRNA, the adaptive walk refinement is still able to solve the puzzle in ~10 minutes, well within the 24 hours allowed by Anderson-Lee et al.12 Furthermore, refinement beginning from GC initialization is sufficient to solve this puzzle with efficiency comparable to that beginning from the SentRNA initialization (Table 4),  demonstrating that the prior information encoded in the refinement moves, and not in the initialization, is primarily responsible for SentRNA's solving of hard Y.  Figure 6: The “zigzag” is an unusual structural motif consisting of two successive length-1 bulges that makes the puzzle hard Y difficult to solve. We found that SentRNA could not learn the stabilization strategy for the zigzag from the training set, and that refinement was necessary to solve the puzzle. This involves swapping one GC pair in the zigzag and mutating the second bulge base of the zigzag to C to prevent mispairing, as well as swapping two GC pairs from the multiloop (highlighted, red). Unnecessary moves are highlighted in blue. 5) Symmetric structural elements Highly symmetric structures are more difficult to design, because the naive strategy of assigning the same base sequences to identical substructures often leads to mispairing between them. Boosting can sufficiently compensate in the case of relatively simple puzzles such as Shortie 4 and Shortie 6, but is insufficient for more complicated puzzles such as Kyurem 5 and Kyurem 7. Puzzles that are even more difficult, such as Mutated chicken feet, require not only asymmetric base assignments, but also additional advanced stabilization strategies. Mutated chicken feet consists of 3 identically structured branches, each consisting of a combination of bulges, short stems, and multiloops. This combination of difficult design elements makes the puzzle especially challenging. To solve this puzzle, in addition to assigning asymmetric bases between branches, we found that it was also necessary to apply an advanced strategy of placing a U in the middle of each 3-loop (Figure 7). This strategy is rarely found in the eternasolves dataset, existing in only five player solutions out of the entire set of 1.8 x 104 player solutions. Therefore, none of our models was initially able to learn it via randomly sampling the training data. However, if we train using only these five solutions, the resulting model, renamed here M8, successfully learns and applies this strategy (Figure 7). We included no long-range features in this model, so all three branches have identical base assignments. Finally, like the case with Mat - Lot 2-2 B, we again used EteRNA rendering in place of RNAplot when training and testing M8, as we found that RNAplot was unable to capture the symmetric nature of the three branches. Remarkably, we found that subsequent refinement beginning from the M8 prediction, which we limited to GC pair mutations, was sufficient to introduce the necessary asymmetries and could quickly solve the puzzle in under 4 minutes, computed by performing 100 independent refinement trials using only GC pair mutations (Table 3). This result demonstrates that the  bottleneck in solving Mutated chicken feet is the mutation of the middle base of the 3-loops to U. If SentRNA can learn this strategy, adding the necessary asymmetries between the three branches can be accomplished quickly via adaptive walk using a simple moveset. On the other hand, if this strategy is not applied in the initial sequence, this puzzle cannot be solved by refinement (Table 4).  Figure 7: The two strategies necessary to solving Mutated chicken feet are 1) placing a U in the middle of each 3-loop, and 2) assigning bases asymmetrically across the three structurally identical branches. The 3-loop U mutation is a rarely seen strategy in the full set of player solutions, but by limiting the training set to only the 5 solutions that utilize this strategy, SentRNA can successfully learn it. If this strategy is present in the initially predicted solution, even if the three branches begin with identical base assignments, the refinement process can introduce the necessary asymmetries and solve the puzzle (highlighted, red). We hypothesized that the model's performance could be further improved if it could also learn to assign bases asymmetrically between branches during training. To test this hypothesis, we retrained the model while selecting a training set of puzzles with similar symmetric structural elements to Mutated chicken feet via the following procedure: first, we performed additional refinement trajectories on a valid solution predicted by the model, keeping track of any new valid solutions that are sampled. A pairwise mutual information calculation was then carried out using the full set of sampled solutions to generate 38 new long-range features that were used to form a new input vector. We then searched for puzzles in eternasolves that contained at least one of these features (29 puzzles total), and retrained the model using 5 player solutions from each of these puzzles. Prior to training, we also manually modified these player solutions such that all instances of 3-loops are boosted to enable the model to learn the 3-loop boosting strategy. The resulting model, renamed here M8* recognizes the existence of adjacent branches in the puzzle using the new long-range features, and can assign bases asymmetrically across branches. Surprisingly, however, M8*, does not show a statistically significant improvement in performance compared to M8. Eref for M8 is 0.359 ± 0.050 over 92 independent training / refinement trials, compared to 0.372 ± 0.068 over 51 trials for M8*. This result suggests that solving Mutated chicken feet requires the application of specific asymmetries that are not well-represented in the training set, and simply assigning bases asymmetrically across the three branches in an uninformed manner does not productively assist puzzle solving.  Summary of SentRNA's performance on selected puzzles Here, we provide a quantitative summary of the results discussed previously. The model name, puzzle solved, refinement moves used, and mean refinement time needed to solve the puzzle are listed in Table 3. Most puzzles can be solved in ~10 minutes or fewer. The notable exception to this trend is Crop circle 2, which required ~1 hour to solve because SentRNA was unable to learn a necessary strategy (i.e. internal loop boosting) during training, resulting in an uninformative initial sequence prediction. Model  Puzzle Kyurem 7 Kyurem 5 "1,2,3and4bulges" Loop next to a Multiloop Crop circle 2  Refinement moves Refinement time (s) M1 Pair mutations 143.5, 177.6 M2 Pair mutations 747.1, 1341.1 M3 Pair mutations 24.8, 26.1 M4 Full 222.4, 300.0 M5 GC pair mutations, 2625.6, 4175.2 boosts M6 Mat - Lot 2-2 B GC pair mutations 138.5, 148.8 M7 hard Y Full 691.1, 1053.1 M8 Mutated chicken feet GC pair mutations 179.2, 222.8 Table 3: Expected time (mean ± standard error) needed by the adaptive walk refinement to sample a correct solution when a given model is solving a specific puzzle from the Eterna100, and the refinement moveset used. In general, if SentRNA can learn the necessary solution strategies during training, refinement is fast and requires a simple moveset (e.g. Mat – Lot 2-2 B). On the other hand, uninformative training data can lead to significantly longer refinement times and the need for more sophisticated movesets (e.g. Crop circle 2). In addition, we performed additional sets of refinement trials for these puzzles beginning from both GC and random initialization, equal to the number performed starting from SentRNA initialization. We then measured the efficacy of each initialization method by the refinement efficacy, Eref. In doing this, we are testing whether SentRNA is making more intelligent initial predictions (i.e. providing more useful prior information) than the more simplistic initialization methods. The results are shown in Table 4. Puzzle Eref, random init. Eref , GC init. Eref, SentRNA init. Kyurem 7 0 0.317, 0.398 0.480, 0.580 Kyurem 5 0 0.020, 0.051 0.079, 0.141 1,2,3and4bulges 0 0 0.904, 0.956 Loop next to a Multiloop 0.044, 0.089 0.080, 0.137 0.234, 0.316 Crop circle 2 0 0.765, 0.885 0.251, 0.399 Mat - Lot 2-2 B 0 0 0.819, 0.880 hard Y 0.020, 0.044 0.101, 0.145 0.076, 0.115 Mutated chicken feet 0 0 0.410, 0.510 Table 4: Refinement efficacies Eref (mean ± standard error) beginning from SentRNA, GC, and random initializations for select Eterna100 puzzles. SentRNA outperforms the other initialization  methods for most puzzles when it can learn the necessary solution strategies during training and as a result solve the puzzle using a simple refinement moveset and small number of refinement moves. On the other hand, GC initialization significantly outperforms SentRNA when key solution strategies are not learned during training (i.e. internal loop boosting for Crop circle 2). Random initialization is universally inferior to the other two methods. We find that the SentRNA initialization results in a statistically significant increase in E ref for almost all puzzles compared to the other initialization methods, indicating that SentRNA initialization can indeed serve as an informative prior from which to begin refinement. On the other hand, GC initialization performs significantly better than SentRNA initialization for Crop circle 2, suggesting that the GC pairing predicted by SentRNA initialization for this puzzle was in fact suboptimal. This result is a consequence of uninformative training data leading to an uninformative initial prediction by SentRNA, and once again highlights that the quality of the training data is key in determining SentRNA’s performance. Future directions: Although SentRNA shows promising initial results, important improvements can be made. First, as we demonstrate in our above discussion, if the strategies necessary to solving a puzzle are not learned from the training data, SentRNA cannot perform well. This was demonstrated most clearly by SentRNA’s relatively poor performance on Crop circle 2, which was a consequence of not learning how to boost its internal loops from the training data. SentRNA’s inability to learn this strategy was due to the scarcity of large internal loops in the eternasolves training set, as well as rare usage of the internal loop boosting strategy. Therefore, supplying a more selective training set of puzzles in which large internal loops are more common and for which boosting is a necessary part of the solution should allow SentRNA to learn the strategy and make more intelligent initial predictions. Alternatively, since SentRNA was eventually able to solve Crop circle 2 during the refinement stage, adding a reinforcement learning component to SentRNA, such that the model can train on the refinement steps themselves, may enable SentRNA to autonomously learn new strategies and improve its initial predictions. In addition, as we have demonstrated, adaptive walk refinement using a set of simple human design moves can prove surprisingly effective starting even from GC initialization (Table 2). These results show that stochastic application of human design moves is by itself a promising strategy for RNA design. We hypothesize that further augmenting the adaptive walk moveset with more advanced strategies, such as zigzag stabilization, could further improve the adaptive walk’s efficacy and SentRNA's overall performance. Finally, because the ultimate goal in solving inverse RNA folding is to assist real-world RNA design, training SentRNA using player solutions whose folds have been judged not only using the in silico ViennaRNA energy function, but also which have been experimentally validated, might allow our agent to more effectively learn strategies for real-world RNA design. In fact, it has already been shown that incorporating human design strategies into a computational agent can allow it to achieve state-of-the-art performance in this regard. Previously, EteRNA developers created a design agent called EteRNABot, which combined a set of 40 player-submitted, experimentally validated rules and strategies into a custom score function, which EteRNABot then tried to optimize using a stochastic search algorithm similar to that of RNAInverse. Through  this approach, EteRNABot could globally outperform RNAInverse and NUPACK on a set of nine difficult design challenges, judged based on consistency with actual experimental synthesis and structure mapping studies.17 We posit that SentRNA, if trained on experimentally validated player solutions, could yield comparable, if not better, results since it learns in a purely data driven manner and can therefore potentially learn a much richer set of strategies from the data instead of relying on a limited set of hand-coded strategies. Conclusion: We present SentRNA, a computational agent for RNA design that consists of a 3-layer fullyconnected neural network trained on player-submitted solutions from the online RNA design game EteRNA. Given a target structure, the agent predicts a RNA sequence that folds into that structure, which is then further refined via a short adaptive walk. We observe that our agent can effectively learn and apply human-like design strategies to solve a variety of difficult puzzles from the Eterna100 that were previously unsolvable by any computational algorithm, solving 80 / 100 puzzles in total. Furthermore, the agent solves puzzles quickly, typically requiring only minutes of refinement beginning from a prediction by an appropriately trained model. Our results demonstrate the power of combining human and computational design strategies into a single agent, and suggests a new paradigm in machine-based RNA design. Acknowledgements: We would like to thank the EteRNA community for providing the training data for SentRNA and making this study possible.
